## Acts 19:13–16 (id: 9435)

Paul’s ministry was effective, but the traveling Jewish exorcists (people who try to drive out evil spirits) had no real power. They used the name of Jesus as if it were a magic word. However, they did not have a personal relationship with him or the indwelling Holy Spirit (God’s Spirit living in believers).

This contrast shows the difference between true faith and empty practice (compare [Matthew 12:27](https://ref.ly/Matt12:27); [Mark 9:38–39](https://ref.ly/Mark9:38-Mark9:39); [Luke 9:49–50](https://ref.ly/Luke9:49-Luke9:50); [11:19](https://ref.ly/Luke11:19)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 19:13-16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Demons (ID: `deity:Demons`); Sceva (ID: `person:Sceva`); Proclaim (ID: `keyterm:Proclaim`); Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`); Authority (ID: `keyterm:Authority.2`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`)

## Acts 19:17–19 (id: 9436)

The people of Ephesus, both Jews and Greeks, noticed the difference between Paul and the Jewish exorcists. This realization caused them to honor and respect the name of the Lord Jesus. Many people confessed and gave up their sinful practices, including magic.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 19:17-19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Magic (ID: `keyterm:Magic`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); Ephesus (ID: `place:Ephesus`); Greeks (ID: `group:Greek`); Javan (ID: `person:Javan`); Scroll (ID: `realia:Scroll`)

## Acts 19:20 (id: 9437)

Luke’s summary of the success of the gospel in Ephesus is short but powerful (compare [2:43–47](https://ref.ly/Acts2:43-Acts2:47); [5:14](https://ref.ly/Acts5:14); [6:7](https://ref.ly/Acts6:7); [9:31](https://ref.ly/Acts9:31); [12:24](https://ref.ly/Acts12:24); [16:5](https://ref.ly/Acts16:5); and [Romans 1:16](https://ref.ly/Rom1:16)). When the Christian faith overcame problems inside the church, such as division, idolatry (worship of false gods), and pagan practices (religious customs not centered on the true God), it spread widely and grew quickly.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 19:20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Authority (ID: `keyterm:Authority.2`)

## Acts 19:21–22 (id: 9438)

* *Paul resolved in the Spirit*:He was probably troubled by what he heard about Corinth (see 1 Corinthians Book Introduction). Instead of going right away, he sent Timothy. Timothy carried a letter, 1 Corinthians, from Paul to the Corinthians ([1 Corinthians 4:17](https://ref.ly/1Cor4:17); [16:10–11](https://ref.ly/1Cor16:10-1Cor16:11)). Paul later traveled to Macedonia and Achaia ([Acts 20:1–3](https://ref.ly/Acts20:1-Acts20:3)).
* *“I must see Rome as well”*: Paul, perhaps inspired by his successful strategy, wanted to share the gospel in the world's most important city (see study note on 16:8).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 19:21-22
* **Associated Articles:** Acts 16:8 (ID: `9401@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

*the Way*: See study note on 9:2.

## Acts 19:23–41 (id: 9439)

The main god worshiped in Ephesus was the Greek goddess Artemis. People believed she was born there, so the city of Ephesus was known as the guardian of her temple.

Each year, the people held large festivals in her honor. These included sports, music, and theater. During these events, people would shout, “Great is Artemis of the Ephesians!”

The temple of Artemis in Ephesus was one of the seven wonders of the ancient world.

As more people became Christians, the worship of Artemis began to decline. This also affected the local economy, since many people earned money from her temple. However, it became clear that Paul and his coworkers had not committed any crime.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 19:23-41

## Acts 19:31 (id: 9445)

*officials of the province of Asia*: These officials, called *Asiarchs* ("rulers of Asia"), were the appointed leaders in the Roman province of Asia. They served as financial supporters of the city and often supported the worship of the emperor. Some of these high\-ranking officials were friends of Paul. Luke shows that the Christian faith also attracted people of high social status.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 19:31

The tradition said that the image (statue) of Artemis had fallen from heaven. This may mean that it was made from a meteor (a rock that falls to earth from space).

## Acts 20:1–2 (id: 9463)

Paul traveled to Macedonia, where he encouraged Christians in towns like Thessalonica, Philippi, and Berea. He also continued collecting donations for needy Christians in Jerusalem (see [Romans 15:25–28](https://ref.ly/Rom15:25-Rom15:28); [2 Corinthians 8:1–9:15](https://ref.ly/2Cor8:1-2Cor9:15)). 

Titus met Paul in Macedonia with news from Corinth, prompting Paul to write 2 Corinthians and send Titus back with the letter ([2 Corinthians 7:5–7](https://ref.ly/2Cor7:5-2Cor7:7); [8:6](https://ref.ly/2Cor8:6)).

## Acts 20:7–12 (id: 9477)

* On the first day of the week, the early church remembered the resurrection of Jesus (see [Mark 16:9](https://ref.ly/Mark16:9); compare [John 20:19](https://ref.ly/John20:19); [Revelation 1:10](https://ref.ly/Rev1:10)).
* This was Paul’s last visit to Troas. The believers met to share the Lord’s Supper (a meal that remembers Jesus’s death). This likely included both the bread and cup (communion) and a shared meal (compare [Acts 2:42](https://ref.ly/Acts2:42), [46](https://ref.ly/Acts2:46); [Jude 1:12](https://ref.ly/Jude1:12)).
* One notable part of this gathering was the quiet miracle involving Eutychus. He was a young man who fell asleep while sitting in a window. He fell three stories and died. Paul went down, bent over him, and took him in his arms. Then Eutychus was restored to life (God brought him back to life through Paul; compare [Acts 9:36–41](https://ref.ly/Acts9:36-Acts9:41)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 20:7-12

## Acts 20:17 (id: 9466)

Miletus was a major sea port on the western coast of Asia Minor at the opening of the Meander River. Paul met briefly with the elders of the church of Ephesus there on his way to Jerusalem.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 20:17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Miletus (ID: `place:Miletus`); Ephesus (ID: `place:Ephesus`); Church (ID: `keyterm:Church`)

## Acts 20:18–38 (id: 9467)

Paul’s message to the elders (leaders) of the church in Ephesus describes his life and work among them. He also calls these leaders to show the same kind of commitment as they continue the ministry.

In this message, Paul points to his own life as an example. He shows his honesty and care for people ([20:18–21](https://ref.ly/Acts20:18-Acts20:21), [26](https://ref.ly/Acts20:26), [31](https://ref.ly/Acts20:31)). He also speaks about what will happen in the future ([20:22–23](https://ref.ly/Acts20:22-Acts20:23), [25](https://ref.ly/Acts20:25), [29–30](https://ref.ly/Acts20:29-Acts20:30)). He warns them about false teachers (people who teach wrong ideas about God; [20:29–30](https://ref.ly/Acts20:29-Acts20:30)). And he urges the elders to stay alert and faithful ([20:28](https://ref.ly/Acts20:28), [31](https://ref.ly/Acts20:31)). 

Through his example, Paul shows what it means to lead as a servant, with sacrifice and careful attention.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 20:18-38

## Acts 20:28 (id: 9471)

* Paul describes the church as God’s people and God’s flock (a group cared for by a shepherd; compare [1 Peter 2:25](https://ref.ly/1Pet2:25); [5:2](https://ref.ly/1Pet5:2), [4](https://ref.ly/1Pet5:4)). In other places, the church is described as:

    + "the body of Christ" ([1 Corinthians 12:27](https://ref.ly/1Cor12:27); [Ephesians 1:23](https://ref.ly/Eph1:23); [4:12](https://ref.ly/Eph4:12); [Colossians 1:24](https://ref.ly/Col1:24));
        + the "bride" of Christ ([2 Corinthians 11:2](https://ref.ly/2Cor11:2); [Revelation 19:7](https://ref.ly/Rev19:7); see [Ephesians 5:25–32](https://ref.ly/Eph5:25-Eph5:32);
        + "God's temple" ([1 Corinthians 3:16](https://ref.ly/1Cor3:16); [2 Corinthians 6:16](https://ref.ly/2Cor6:16)).
        + "a chosen people, a royal priesthood, a holy nation, a people for God’s own possession" ([1 Peter 2:9](https://ref.ly/1Pet2:9)); and
        + "God's field, God's building" ([1 Corinthians 3:9](https://ref.ly/1Cor3:9)).

* Paul expected the leaders to care for and guide the church, like shepherds care for their flock. The Holy Spirit had appointed them to this role(see [1 Timothy 3:1–7](https://ref.ly/1Tim3:1-1Tim3:7); [Titus 1:5–7](https://ref.ly/Titus1:5-Titus1:7); [1 Peter 5:1–4](https://ref.ly/1Pet5:1-1Pet5:4); compare [Acts 6:2–4](https://ref.ly/Acts6:2-Acts6:4)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 20:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Holy Spirit (ID: `deity:HolySpirit`); Church (ID: `keyterm:Church`); Blood (ID: `keyterm:Blood`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Acts 21:1 (id: 9482)

* Cos was an island in the Aegean Sea with a major trade port.
* Rhodes is a large Aegean island. The Colossus, a huge statue 30 meters (100 feet) tall, once stood at the city's entrance. In Paul’s time, the statue lay where it had fallen during an earthquake over 200 years earlier. It would remain there for another 600 years.
* Patara was the main port of Lycia, located on the coast across from Rhodes.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cos (ID: `place:Cos`); Rhodes (ID: `place:Rhodes`); Patara (ID: `place:Patara`)

## Acts 21:2–3 (id: 9483)

Tyre was an important port in Phoenicia with an empire centered on sea activity and widespread trade interests (see [Isaiah 23](https://ref.ly/Isa23:1-Isa23:18); [Jeremiah 25:15–38](https://ref.ly/Jer25:15-Jer25:38); [47](https://ref.ly/Jer47:1-Jer47:7); [Zechariah 9](https://ref.ly/Zech9:1-Zech9:17); [Matthew 15:21–28](https://ref.ly/Matt15:21-Matt15:28); [Mark 7:24–31](https://ref.ly/Mark7:24-Mark7:31)).

## Acts 21:4–6 (id: 9491)

* The disciples in Tyre gave Paul a warm and emotional farewell. This shows the deep fellowship (shared life and unity) among Christians.
* *Through the Spirit they kept telling Paul not to go up to Jerusalem**:* The disciples in Tyre clearly understood the danger he would face there. Because they cared for him, they tried to persuade him not to go.

    However, Paul accepted these risks. He chose to go in order to complete the mission God had given him (compare [Philippians 3:7–10](https://ref.ly/Phil3:7-Phil3:10)). See also study note on Acts 21:11–14.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:4-6
* **Associated Articles:** Acts 21:11-14 (ID: `9480@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Acts 21:9 (id: 9494)

Philip’s daughters had the gift of prophecy (the ability to speak messages from God). This showed that Joel’s prophecy ([Joel 2:28–32](https://ref.ly/Joel2:28-Joel2:32)) had been fulfilled, just as Peter had said at Pentecost ([2:17–21](https://ref.ly/Acts2:17-Acts2:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`)

## Acts 21:10 (id: 9479)

Luke places female prophets ([21:9](https://ref.ly/Acts21:9)) alongside a male prophet (see study note on 17:34). This shows that both women and men were used by God to speak his message in the early church.

Agabus, like the Old Testament prophets (God’s messengers before the time of Jesus), used symbolic actions (visible actions that show a message) to communicate what God had revealed to him (see “Prophetic Sign Acts” Theme Note).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:10
* **Associated Articles:** Acts 17:34 (ID: `9413@TyndaleStudyNotes`); Prophetic Sign Acts (ID: `500313@TyndaleStudyNotesThemes`)

## Acts 21:11–14 (id: 9480)

Despite Agabus’s prediction of suffering and the believers’ begging Paul not to go to Jerusalem, Paul was ready even to die for the sake of the Lord Jesus. Luke emphasizes Paul’s courage, determination, and heroism as a Christian missionary who would let nothing interfere with his mission. See study note on 21:4–6.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:11-14

## Acts 21:18–19 (id: 9481)

It was important for James and all the elders (leaders) of the church in Jerusalem to hear about Paul’s successful work among the Gentiles. It was also important for the Jerusalem church, the first main church, to continue to support this mission ([21:20](https://ref.ly/Acts21:20); see [15:7–21](https://ref.ly/Acts15:7-Acts15:21)). 

Paul’s report showed that God had carried out his plan among the Gentiles through Paul’s work.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:18-19

## Acts 21:20–25 (id: 9484)

The leaders of the Jerusalem church urged Paul to strengthen his credibility. They encouraged him to demonstrate that he was not teaching Jews to abandon the laws of Moses. Meanwhile, the leaders did not force Jewish rules on Gentile converts. They had determined these terms earlier and would respect them ([15:22–29](https://ref.ly/Acts15:22-Acts15:29)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:20-25

The concerns of the Christian leaders were well\-founded ([21:20–25](https://ref.ly/Acts21:20-Acts21:25)). ## Acts 21:28–29 (id: 9486)

*Greeks*: It was a crime punishable by death to bring Gentiles (non\-Jewish people) into the inner areas of the temple, beyond the Court of the Gentiles (see also Josephus, *War* 5\.5\.2; 6\.2\.4\). The Jews believed Paul broke this holy law by bringing Trophimus, a Gentile from Ephesus, into the area reserved for only Jews (see also [Acts 20:4](https://ref.ly/Acts20:4); [2 Timothy 4:20](https://ref.ly/2Tim4:20)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:28-29

Herod the Great built the Antonia Fortress, a large military base, on the northwest corner of the Temple Mount. It housed the Roman soldiers stationed there to keep the peace.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Something Definite (ID: `keyterm:SomethingDefinite`)

## Acts 21:37–40 (id: 9490)

The commander had mistaken Paul for an Egyptian false messiah who had planned to seize power from the Romans around AD 54 (roughly three years earlier; see Josephus, *War* 2\.13\.5\). Paul corrected the mistake, gained permission to speak to the people, and addressed the crowd in Aramaic, the common language of Judea. He gave a strong statement of his faith in Jesus as the Messiah ([Acts 22:1–21](https://ref.ly/Acts22:1-Acts22:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:37-40

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Tarsus (ID: `place:Tarsus`); Cilicia (ID: `place:Cilicia`); Tarsians (ID: `group:Tarsus`); Military Member (ID: `person:MilitaryMember`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Be Lawful (ID: `keyterm:Lawful`); Step (ID: `realia:Step`)

## Acts 22:1–21 (id: 9495)

Paul gives his most complete defense of his life and faith before his own people in Jerusalem. This speech shows his flexibility as a missionary, just as his message to the Greek philosophers in Athens also did ([17:22–31](https://ref.ly/Acts17:22-Acts17:31); see [1 Corinthians 9:20–23](https://ref.ly/1Cor9:20-1Cor9:23)). 

He begins by showing his close connection with his audience. He explains his Jewish background and his training under Gamaliel the Elder (a respected Jewish teacher). He also describes his strong desire to honor God, a desire they shared ([Acts 22:1–3](https://ref.ly/Acts22:1-Acts22:3)). 

Paul then recounts how he once persecuted Christians ([22:4–5](https://ref.ly/Acts22:4-Acts22:5)). He tells about seeing Jesus on the road to Damascus ([22:6–10](https://ref.ly/Acts22:6-Acts22:10)). and explains his conversion (his turning to follow Jesus; [22:11–16](https://ref.ly/Acts22:11-Acts22:16)). 

He ends by describing a vision in the temple, where the Lord spoke to him. The Lord told him that many Jews would reject the message and that he should go to the Gentiles ([22:17–21](https://ref.ly/Acts22:17-Acts22:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:1-21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Damascus (ID: `place:Damascus`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Bear Witness (ID: `keyterm:BearWitness`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Law (ID: `keyterm:Law`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Witness (ID: `keyterm:Witness`); Ananias (ID: `person:Ananias.2`); Gamaliel (ID: `person:Gamaliel`); Tarsus (ID: `place:Tarsus`); Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); Pray (ID: `keyterm:Pray`); Cilicia (ID: `place:Cilicia`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`); Stephen (ID: `person:Stephen`); Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`); Blood (ID: `keyterm:Blood`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); Nazareth (ID: `place:Nazareth`); Call Upon (ID: `keyterm:CallUpon`); Baptism (ID: `keyterm:Baptism`); Synagogue (ID: `realia:Synagogue`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`); Cloak (ID: `realia:Cloak`); Prison (ID: `realia:Prison`)

## Acts 22:17–22 (id: 9499)

While Paul was praying in the temple*,* he saw a vision of Jesus. Jesus told him that the people of Jerusalem would not accept his message. The Lord then sent him to the Gentiles. The crowd’s reaction to this report confirmed this reality ([22:22–23](https://ref.ly/Acts22:22-Acts22:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:17-22

## Acts 22:23 (id: 9500)

The crowd shouted, threw off their coats, and tossed dust into the air. These actions were likely ritual responses to what they thought was blasphemy (speech that dishonors God). They strongly opposed Paul’s message, especially his claim that Gentiles were included among God's people ([22:21](https://ref.ly/Acts22:21)). Compare with [Luke 4:16–30](https://ref.ly/Luke4:16-Luke4:30).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cloak (ID: `realia:Cloak`)

## Acts 22:25–29 (id: 9501)

Paul asserted his Roman citizenship at a crucial moment when he encountered torture to make him confess a supposed crime. Roman citizenship was valuable. Claiming it falsely was a capital offense. Its main benefits included protection from whipping and the right to appeal to the emperor ([25:11](https://ref.ly/Acts25:11)). The commander was afraid because he had almost violated Roman law (compare [16:35–39](https://ref.ly/Acts16:35-Acts16:39)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:25-29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Military Member (ID: `person:MilitaryMember`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Be Lawful (ID: `keyterm:Lawful`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Strap (ID: `realia:Strap`)

## Acts 22:28 (id: 9502)

*I paid a high price*: During the early rule of Roman emperor Claudius (AD 41–54\), people could buy Roman citizenship, but it was expensive.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Military Member (ID: `person:MilitaryMember`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)

## Acts 23:1 (id: 9507)

When speaking to the Jewish high council (the Sanhedrin), Paul said he had lived with a clear conscience before God. He had not broken God’s law or done what they accused him of doing.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Good (ID: `keyterm:Good`); Live (ID: `keyterm:Live.3`); Conscience (ID: `keyterm:Conscience`)

## Acts 23:2 (id: 9511)

* Ananias was the Jewish high priest from AD 47 to 58\.
* *strike him:* Ananias had the apostle struck because he thought Paul was lying and wanted to scare him.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ananias (ID: `person:Ananias.3`)

## Acts 23:3 (id: 9514)

*you whitewashed wall*: Compare [Ezekiel 13:10–17](https://ref.ly/Ezek13:10-Ezek13:17); [Matthew 23:27](https://ref.ly/Matt23:27).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Disobey (ID: `keyterm:Disobey`); Law (ID: `keyterm:Law`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)

## Acts 23:5 (id: 9515)

* It is unclear why Paul did not recognize the high priest.
* Paul responded by quoting [Exodus 22:28](https://ref.ly/Exod22:28), showing respect for the high priest's position.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)

## Acts 23:6 (id: 9516)

Paul focused on the main issue in his trial: the hope of the resurrection (the belief that the dead will be raised to life). His preaching came from this hope and from the fact that Jesus had risen from the dead.

However, his message was not accepted by either group of Jews because of what it meant. The Pharisees believed in the resurrection, but they could not accept that Gentiles could be included among God’s people without circumcision and without keeping the law of Moses (see [15:5](https://ref.ly/Acts15:5)). Yet this is exactly what the resurrection of Jesus and the gift of the Holy Spirit had made possible (see [Acts 2:39](https://ref.ly/Acts2:39); [10:34–48](https://ref.ly/Acts10:34-Acts10:48)). 

The Sadducees, on the other hand, did not believe in the resurrection at all. Because of this, they strongly opposed any teaching about the resurrection (see [4:1–2](https://ref.ly/Acts4:1-Acts4:2)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`); Pharisees (ID: `group:Pharisee`); Sadducees (ID: `group:Sadducee`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`)

## Acts 23:7–10 (id: 9517)

Paul's statement divided the Sanhedrin ([23:6](https://ref.ly/Acts23:6)). The Pharisees supported Paul against the Sadducees. The disagreement was so intense that the commander rescued Paul and took him back to the fortress of Antonia.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:7-10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pharisees (ID: `group:Pharisee`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Sadducees (ID: `group:Sadducee`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`)

## Acts 23:11 (id: 9504)

At this important moment, Jesus encouraged Paul to continue as his faithful witness by assuring him that he would go to Rome (see [19:21](https://ref.ly/Acts19:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Cheer Up (ID: `keyterm:CheerUp`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Declare (ID: `keyterm:Declare`); Bear Witness (ID: `keyterm:BearWitness`)

## Acts 23:12–15 (id: 9505)

The plan to kill Paul was desperate. More than 40 men took an oath (a strong promise) not to eat or drink until they had killed him (compare [1 Samuel 14:24–46](https://ref.ly/1Sam14:24-1Sam14:46); [Matthew 14:6–11](https://ref.ly/Matt14:6-Matt14:11); see also [Deuteronomy 23:21–23](https://ref.ly/Deut23:21-Deut23:23); [Matthew 5:33–37](https://ref.ly/Matt5:33-Matt5:37); [James 5:12](https://ref.ly/Jas5:12)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:12-15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Decision (ID: `keyterm:Decision`)

## Acts 23:16–22 (id: 9506)

Paul’s nephew stopped the plan to kill him by telling a Roman officer.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:16-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Ask for (ID: `keyterm:AskFor`); Military Member (ID: `person:MilitaryMember`); Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Promise (ID: `keyterm:Promise`)

## Acts 23:23–35 (id: 9508)

A large group riding horses safely brought Paul to the Roman governor Felix in Caesarea, the Roman headquarters for Judea. Paul would have more protection there than in Jerusalem. The soldiers carried out the mission that night with great care and secrecy ([23:31](https://ref.ly/Acts23:31)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:23-35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Caesarea (ID: `place:Caesarea`); Lysias (ID: `person:Lysias`); Cilicia (ID: `place:Cilicia`); Antipatris (ID: `place:Antipatris`); Herod (ID: `person:Herod`); Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Law (ID: `keyterm:Law`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Rescue (ID: `keyterm:Rescue`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); Palace (ID: `realia:Palace`)

## Acts 23:24 (id: 9509)

Antonius Felix was the governor of Judea from about AD 52 to 59\. He was in charge of both military and civil matters. Felix had a bad reputation and was eventually called back to Rome by Emperor Nero (see [24:24–27](https://ref.ly/Acts24:24-Acts24:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)

## Acts 23:26–30 (id: 9510)

* The letter from Claudius Lysias to Governor Felix follows the common form of a Hellenistic letter (a standard Greek\-style letter used in that time). It names the writer and the recipient, gives a greeting, and explains the main issue. However, it does not include a closing farewell (see study note on 15:29).

    The letter also summarizes the events leading up to it and explains the actions taken. It shows that the commander believed he had followed proper Roman legal procedures.

* *His Excellency*: This title was often used for people with high social, political, or economic status ([Luke 1:3](https://ref.ly/Luke1:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:26-30
* **Associated Articles:** Acts 15:29 (ID: `9373@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lysias (ID: `person:Lysias`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Rescue (ID: `keyterm:Rescue`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Law (ID: `keyterm:Law`)

## Acts 23:31 (id: 9512)

Antipatris was a city rebuilt by Herod the Great in 9 BC on the Plain of Sharon. It served as a strategic military control point between Jerusalem and Caesarea.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Antipatris (ID: `place:Antipatris`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)

## Acts 23:35 (id: 9513)

* The governor followed the correct steps and waited for Paul's accusers to arrive before having an official legal hearing.
* *Herod's Praetorium:* Herod’s headquarters was the palace built by Herod the Great in Caesarea. Later, this palace became the residence (official home) of the Roman governors of Judea.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Palace (ID: `realia:Palace`); Herod (ID: `person:Herod`)

## Acts 24:1–4 (id: 9519)

Tertullus presented the case for the prosecution. He began with the usual words of praise to gain the governor’s attention and favor. Then he listed the charges against Paul ([24:5–8](https://ref.ly/Acts24:5-Acts24:8)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:1-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Tertullus (ID: `person:Tertullus`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Ananias (ID: `person:Ananias.3`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Ask for (ID: `keyterm:AskFor`); Make Peace (ID: `keyterm:Peace`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`)

## Acts 24:1–27 (id: 9518)

Tertullus presented a legal case against Paul in a Roman court on behalf of the high priest ([24:1–9](https://ref.ly/Acts24:1-Acts24:9)). Then Paul cheerfully defended himself and his faith ([24:10–21](https://ref.ly/Acts24:10-Acts24:21)). The governor paused the hearing without making a decision. He kept Paul in prison for two years ([24:22–27](https://ref.ly/Acts24:22-Acts24:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:1-27

## Acts 24:5 (id: 9531)

* *a pestilence, stirring up dissension among the Jews all over the world*: A Roman court would have taken this charge very seriously. This language presents him as someone who causes unrest. It reflects a charge of political sedition (acting against the state), which a Roman court would have treated as a serious crime (see [16:21](https://ref.ly/Acts16:21); [17:7](https://ref.ly/Acts17:7); [18:13](https://ref.ly/Acts18:13)). People made similar accusations against Jesus to Pontius Pilate ([Luke 23:2](https://ref.ly/Luke23:2), [5](https://ref.ly/Luke23:5), [14](https://ref.ly/Luke23:14)).
* *sect:* This term is used here in a negative way. It suggests that Paul's faith was suspicious or even unlawful (see also [Acts 24:14](https://ref.ly/Acts24:14)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Nazareth (ID: `place:Nazareth`)

## Acts 24:15 (id: 9522)

At the final judgment (the time when God judges all people), God will raise both the righteous (those who follow him) and the unrighteous (those who do not). Paul kept this coming meeting with God in mind at all times ([24:16](https://ref.ly/Acts24:16)). Felix became afraid when Paul spoke about “the coming judgment” in a later conversation ([24:25](https://ref.ly/Acts24:25)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:15

## Acts 24:16 (id: 9523)

Paul emphasized that he had acted with a clear conscience (see [20:27](https://ref.ly/Acts20:27), [33](https://ref.ly/Acts20:33); [23:1](https://ref.ly/Acts23:1); [1 Corinthians 4:4](https://ref.ly/1Cor4:4); [2 Corinthians 1:12](https://ref.ly/2Cor1:12); [4:2](https://ref.ly/2Cor4:2); see also [2 Timothy 1:3](https://ref.ly/2Tim1:3)). He remained true to his Jewish heritage and did not fear God's judgment ([Acts 24:14,](https://ref.ly/Acts24:14) [15](https://ref.ly/Acts24:15)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Conscience (ID: `keyterm:Conscience`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Acts 24:23 (id: 9526)

Proper treatment of a Roman citizen involved giving him some freedom and allowing his friends to visit and care for him.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Serve (ID: `keyterm:Serve`)

## Acts 24:24 (id: 9527)

Drusilla was the sister of Herod Agrippa II and Bernice ([25:13](https://ref.ly/Acts25:13)). She left her first husband, Azizus, the King of Emessa, to marry Felix. Drusilla was Jewish, so by leaving her original husband and marrying Felix, she violated God's commands ([Exodus 20:14](https://ref.ly/Exod20:14); [Deuteronomy 5:18](https://ref.ly/Deut5:18); compare [Malachi 2:16](https://ref.ly/Mal2:16); [Mark 10:12](https://ref.ly/Mark10:12)). The Jewish historian Josephus criticized her for doing this (*Antiquities* 20\.7\.1–2\). During their conversation with Paul, Drusilla and her husband were faced with the reality of God’s judgment (his just decision about human actions).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Drusilla (ID: `person:Drusilla`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`)

## Acts 24:25 (id: 9528)

Paul's words about righteousness, self\-control, and the coming judgment scared Felix, who was notably corrupt ([24:26–27](https://ref.ly/Acts24:26-Acts24:27); see study notes on 23:24; 24:24).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:25
* **Associated Articles:** Acts 23:24 (ID: `9509@TyndaleStudyNotes`); Acts 24:24 (ID: `9527@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judgment (ID: `keyterm:Judgment`); Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`)

## Acts 24:26–27 (id: 9529)

Felix kept Paul in custody, hoping that Paul would offer him a bribe. When this did not work and his term as governor ended, he left Paul in prison to please the Jewish people.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:26-27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Acts 24:27–25.5 (id: 9530)

Felix’s successor was Porcius Festus. Nero appointed him as governor of Judea around AD 59–62\. Josephus describes Festus as a careful and honest leader. However, he could not stop the growing unrest among the Jewish people, even though he took strong action against the Assassins (a violent group also called the Sicarii; Josephus, *Antiquities* 20\.8\.10; see also [Acts 21:38](https://ref.ly/Acts21:38)). 

Festus refused the Jewish leaders’ request to move Paul’s trial to Jerusalem, but he still felt their pressure ([25:9](https://ref.ly/Acts25:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:27-25:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Caesarea (ID: `place:Caesarea`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Ask for (ID: `keyterm:AskFor`)

## Acts 25:7 (id: 9540)

The serious accusations were likely the ones mentioned earlier ([21:27–28](https://ref.ly/Acts21:27-Acts21:28); [24:5–9](https://ref.ly/Acts24:5-Acts24:9)). But there was no evidence to support them ([24:10–13](https://ref.ly/Acts24:10-Acts24:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`)

## Acts 25:9 (id: 9541)

*Festus, wishing to do the Jews a favor*: The governor later gave an official reason for delaying Paul's trial and asking to move it to Jerusalem ([25:17–20](https://ref.ly/Acts25:17-Acts25:20)). However, politics, not justice, motivated his request.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Festus (ID: `person:Festus`)

## Acts 25:10–11 (id: 9533)

Festus suggested that Paul have his trial in Jerusalem, which led Paul to appeal to Caesar. Paul did not fear death, but he opposed being transferred to a court biased toward condemnation, not justice ([25:7](https://ref.ly/Acts25:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:10-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Claudius (ID: `person:Claudius`); To Act Wickedly (ID: `keyterm:ActWickedly`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Call Upon (ID: `keyterm:CallUpon`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Judgment Seat (ID: `realia:JudgmentSeat`)

## Acts 25:12 (id: 9534)

Festus approved Paul's request. This confirmed Paul's belief that he must visit Rome ([19:21](https://ref.ly/Acts19:21); see also [23:11](https://ref.ly/Acts23:11); [27:24](https://ref.ly/Acts27:24); [Romans 1:13–15](https://ref.ly/Rom1:13-Rom1:15); [15:22–29](https://ref.ly/Rom15:22-Rom15:29)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Claudius (ID: `person:Claudius`); Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Call Upon (ID: `keyterm:CallUpon`)

## Acts 25:13–22 (id: 9535)

Festus discussed Paul’s case with Herod Agrippa II, who came to Caesarea to pay a courtesy visit to the new governor. Herod Agrippa II ruled parts of the region from AD 50–100\. 

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:13-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Agrippa (ID: `person:Agrippa`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Augustus (ID: `person:Augustus`); Bernice (ID: `person:Bernice`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Caesarea (ID: `place:Caesarea`); Decision (ID: `keyterm:Decision`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Claudius (ID: `person:Claudius`); Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Call Upon (ID: `keyterm:CallUpon`); Condemn (ID: `keyterm:Condemn`); Judgment Seat (ID: `realia:JudgmentSeat`)

## Acts 25:16 (id: 9536)

This was a fundamental principle: Roman law did not convict people without a trial. They must be given an opportunity to confront their accusers and defend themselves (see [19:38–39](https://ref.ly/Acts19:38-Acts19:39)). This showed Roman legal procedures in a positive way.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`)

## Acts 25:17 (id: 9537)

*I did not delay*: This is different from Felix, the previous governor, who delayed making a decision ([24:22–27](https://ref.ly/Acts24:22-Acts24:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judgment Seat (ID: `realia:JudgmentSeat`)

## Acts 25:18–20 (id: 9538)

These verses provide the official reasons Festus gave for his actions (see also [25:9](https://ref.ly/Acts25:9)). There were no criminal charges against Paul. The objections were about their religion and focused on Paul's claim that Jesus is alive.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:18-20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)

## Acts 25:23–27 (id: 9539)

Paul's legal hearing before King Agrippa included all the ceremony of a royal visit. The main purpose was for Agrippa to advise Festus on what to write in the appeal to Caesar. There was no clear charge against Paul. Festus believed Paul had done nothing deserving death.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:23-27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Agrippa (ID: `person:Agrippa`); Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Bernice (ID: `person:Bernice`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Call Upon (ID: `keyterm:CallUpon`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Discern (ID: `keyterm:Discern`); Augustus (ID: `person:Augustus`); Audience Hall (ID: `realia:AudienceHall`)

## Acts 26:1–23 (id: 9542)

In his careful defense before King Agrippa, Paul argued that his message agreed fully with the Jewish faith. He began by respectfully recognizing Agrippa’s knowledge of Jewish matters ([26:2–3](https://ref.ly/Acts26:2-Acts26:3)). He then described his early life, his training in Judaism, and his membership in the Pharisees (a Jewish group known for strict obedience to the law; [26:4–5](https://ref.ly/Acts26:4-Acts26:5)). 

Paul explained that the charges against him were only because he believed that God had fulfilled the Jewish hope of the resurrection (God raising the dead to life; [26:6–8](https://ref.ly/Acts26:6-Acts26:8)). 

He then told how he had once strongly opposed Christians ([26:9–11](https://ref.ly/Acts26:9-Acts26:11)), but changed after seeing a vision from Jesus on the road to Damascus ([26:12–18](https://ref.ly/Acts26:12-Acts26:18); see [9:1–18](https://ref.ly/Acts9:1-Acts9:18)). 

Paul said that his preaching simply obeyed this message from God ([26:19–20](https://ref.ly/Acts26:19-Acts26:20)). Although some Jews opposed him violently ([26:21\)](https://ref.ly/Acts26:21), God protected him. He continued to teach a message that his fellow Jews should have accepted [(](https://ref.ly/Acts26:21)[22–23](https://ref.ly/Acts26:22-Acts26:23)). This defense shows an example for Christians who are put on trial for their faith (see [9:15](https://ref.ly/Acts9:15); [Luke 21:12–15](https://ref.ly/Luke21:12-Luke21:15)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:1-23

## Acts 26:26 (id: 9547)

*it was not done in a corner*: The main events of the Christian faith were public historical events. Witnesses could confirm them as true. King Agrippa could not contradict Paul's statements of fact.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`); Cornerstone (ID: `realia:Cornerstone`)

## Acts 26:27–28 (id: 9548)

Paul's question put King Agrippa in a difficult position. If Agrippa said he believed the prophets, Paul would press the Christian message. If Agrippa denied belief in the prophets, he would upset the devout Jews who were listening. 

Agrippa knew Paul was not crazy and that Paul's testimony about Jesus was historically accurate ([26:26](https://ref.ly/Acts26:26)). So, Agrippa avoided answering Paul's question and refused to consider the claims of Christ. Agrippa said that Paul's statement was too short for him to make a responsible decision.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:27-28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Agrippa (ID: `person:Agrippa`); Christians (ID: `group:Christ`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`)

## Acts 26:28 (id: 9549)

*"**Can you persuade me in such a short time to become a Christian?**"*: This remark might have been ironic, expressing a lack of belief. It might have been making fun of Paul, or a dismissal of Paul's challenge. It might also have been a comment on how well Paul could persuade. Or it could have been a genuine question about Paul's intention. It seems best to understand King Agrippa's reply as intentionally trying to avoid the question. He did not want to admit he believed the prophets, because Paul had made a strong case ([26:27](https://ref.ly/Acts26:27)). 

The next step would be to believe in Jesus as the promised Christ, which Agrippa did not want to do. On the other hand, he did not want to say he did not believe the prophets. This would cause his Jewish subjects to feel isolated, and Agrippa wanted their allegiance. His lack of a committed response emphasizes his discomfort with Paul's testimony.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Christians (ID: `group:Christ`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Agrippa (ID: `person:Agrippa`)

## Acts 26:29 (id: 9550)

Paul’s bold answer shows that he could respond quickly and clearly. He challenges Agrippa and the whole audience to think about the value of knowing Christ and making a personal commitment to him.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Chain (ID: `realia:Chain`)

## Acts 26:31 (id: 9551)

The rulers agreed that Paul did not deserve death or imprisonment. Roman authorities repeatedly gave this verdict when considering Paul's case ([25:25](https://ref.ly/Acts25:25); see [Luke 23:4](https://ref.ly/Luke23:4), [15](https://ref.ly/Luke23:15), [22](https://ref.ly/Luke23:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:31


## Acts 26:32 (id: 9552)

Paul could have been set free because the legal verdict was evident ([26:31](https://ref.ly/Acts26:31)). However, if he had not appealed to Caesar, Paul might not have survived ([25:1–11](https://ref.ly/Acts25:1-Acts25:11)). By appealing, he was fulfilling God's purposes for him ([23:11](https://ref.ly/Acts23:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:32

## Acts 28:3–6 (id: 9587)

The people of Malta believed that justice was a personal power or a god who punished criminals. When nothing bad happened to Paul, they changed their minds. They saw that he was not harmed by the snake. So they thought he had power over snakes and said that he must be a god (compare [14:11–12](https://ref.ly/Acts14:11-Acts14:12)).

But this was not true. Paul did not have special power in himself. Instead, his survival showed that God was protecting him (compare [Mark 16:17–18](https://ref.ly/Mark16:17-Mark16:18)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:3-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); Snake (ID: `fauna:Snake`); Cobra (ID: `fauna:Cobra`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)

## Acts 28:17–20 (id: 9580)

Paul knew that the gospel should be shared with the Jews first ([13:46](https://ref.ly/Acts13:46); [Romans 1:16](https://ref.ly/Rom1:16)). He was also concerned that false charges against him might have already reached Rome. So he called together the local Jewish leaders and explained his life and work. He insisted that he had not committed any crime. 

But strong opposition from other Jews had forced him to appeal to the emperor. Paul said he had nothing against his own people. Instead, he wanted to explain his deep conviction that the Messiah (God’s promised king) they were waiting for had already come in Jesus of Nazareth.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:17-20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Claudius (ID: `person:Claudius`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`); Call Upon (ID: `keyterm:CallUpon`); Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Discern (ID: `keyterm:Discern`); Ask for (ID: `keyterm:AskFor`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Chain (ID: `realia:Chain`)

## Acts 28:21–22 (id: 9582)

The Jewish leaders assured Paul that they had received no reports against him, and they wanted to hear his explanation of this movement.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:21-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Think (ID: `keyterm:Think`); Judea (ID: `place:Judea`)

## Acts 28:23 (id: 9583)

Paul explained how Jesus fulfilled the Old Testament hopes for the kingdom of God. This was the main theme of the preaching of Jesus ([Mark 1:14–15](https://ref.ly/Mark1:14-Mark1:15); see [Matthew 4:12–17](https://ref.ly/Matt4:12-Matt4:17); [Luke 4:14–21](https://ref.ly/Luke4:14-Luke4:21), [43](https://ref.ly/Luke4:43)). Paul used the Old Testament Scriptures to show that Jesus was the promised Messiah.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Law (ID: `keyterm:Law`); Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); Declare (ID: `keyterm:Declare`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Guest Room (ID: `realia:GuestRoom`)

## Acts 28:24 (id: 9584)

Paul spoke to them all day, but the people responded in different ways, just as people had done in other places (for example, [13:40–51](https://ref.ly/Acts13:40-Acts13:51); [17:11–14](https://ref.ly/Acts17:11-Acts17:14)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Disbelieve (ID: `keyterm:Disbelieve`); Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`)

## Acts 28:25–28 (id: 9585)

As Paul left, he gave a warning from Scripture. These words are often used in the New Testament to explain why many Jews did not accept the good news about Jesus (compare [Matthew 13:14–15](https://ref.ly/Matt13:14-Matt13:15); [Mark 4:12](https://ref.ly/Mark4:12); [Luke 8:10](https://ref.ly/Luke8:10); [John 12:38–40](https://ref.ly/John12:38-John12:40); see [Romans 11:1–12](https://ref.ly/Rom11:1-Rom11:12), [25–32](https://ref.ly/Rom11:25-Rom11:32)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:25-28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Holy Spirit (ID: `deity:HolySpirit`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Isaiah (ID: `person:Isaiah`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); Word (ID: `keyterm:Word`); Heart (ID: `keyterm:Heart`)

## Acts 28:28 (id: 9586)

Since Jews everywhere had the chance to accept the faith, it was now time to offer this salvation to the Gentiles ([13:46](https://ref.ly/Acts13:46); see [Romans 1:16](https://ref.ly/Rom1:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`)

## Acts 28:31 (id: 9588)

* Even though Paul was under house arrest, he boldly proclaimed the kingdom message (the good news about God’s rule; [28:16](https://ref.ly/Acts28:16)).
* *freely* (Greek *akōlutōs*, meaning “without restriction”): This Greek word is the last word in the book of Acts and helps show its meaning. God’s word cannot be chained, even when his messengers are ([2 Timothy 2:9](https://ref.ly/2Tim2:9); see [Philippians 1:12–14](https://ref.ly/Phil1:12-Phil1:14)).

    The book of Acts tells the story of a message of good news that extends to everyone worldwide. This includes Jews and Gentiles (non\-Jews), converts to Judaism, the rich, and the poor. The mission to spread this message is done through the power of the Holy Spirit ([Acts 1:8](https://ref.ly/Acts1:8)).

    It reaches:

    + Jews ([3:1–5:42](https://ref.ly/Acts3:1-Acts5:42)),
        + Samaritans ([8:1–25](https://ref.ly/Acts8:1-Acts8:25)),
        + converts to Judaism ([2:11](https://ref.ly/Acts2:11); [13:43](https://ref.ly/Acts13:43)),
        + “God\-fearers” ([8:26–40](https://ref.ly/Acts8:26-Acts8:40); [9:32–11:18](https://ref.ly/Acts9:32-Acts11:18)), and
        + Gentiles ([13:1–28:28](https://ref.ly/Acts13:1-Acts28:28)).

* Luke ends his account with Paul still under house arrest in Rome (around AD 60–62\). They later released Paul and he traveled again. Tradition says Paul was imprisoned again in Rome around AD 64\. He died as a martyr (a witness for Christ) during Emperor Nero’s persecution of Christians.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Proclaim (ID: `keyterm:Proclaim`)
